Um, hi. I guess I came in because I feel like I’m kind of drowning in trying to keep things organized. Like, I’m doing schoolwork, and I also have this little accessories shop, and I can’t seem to keep both of them straight at the same time.

It sounds simple when I say it, but it’s like… my supplies for the shop are in one pile, then receipts are somewhere else, and then my assignment notes and due dates get mixed in with everything. I’ll write something down and then forget where I put it, or I’ll have all these things in front of me and I genuinely don’t know what I’m supposed to do first. Then I just move stuff around or start one thing and remember another thing, and it all gets messy again.

It’s been really frustrating because I’m trying to handle it, but it feels like my school stuff and my shop stuff are constantly spilling into each other. I think that’s the main reason I wanted to talk to someone.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail myself from being little, but my mom actually kept some of my elementary school papers and notes from teachers, and she’s shown them to me before. A lot of them say things like I was always getting out of my seat, or I’d talk before I was called on, or I’d jump into things too fast during group activities.

The funny thing is, it wasn’t usually that I didn’t understand the work. From what my mom says, and from the notes, I’d know what to do, but I’d rush through it or kind of take over in the group before listening to the directions all the way. So, yeah, looking back, it seems like there were signs even then, especially at school.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n2\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF90.0, F41.9, F43.2, F42, F32.9\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I came in because I feel like I’m kind of drowning in trying to keep things organized. Like, I’m doing schoolwork, and I also have this little accessories shop, and I can’t seem to keep both of them straight at the same time.\n\nIt sounds simple when I say it, but it’s like… my supplies for the shop are in one pile, then receipts are somewhere else, and then my assignment notes and due dates get mixed in with everything. I’ll write something down and then forget where I put it, or I’ll have all these things in front of me and I genuinely don’t know what I’m supposed to do first. Then I just move stuff around or start one thing and remember another thing, and it all gets messy again.\n\nIt’s been really frustrating because I’m trying to handle it, but it feels like my school stuff and my shop stuff are constantly spilling into each other. I think that’s the main reason I wanted to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Did you have the same kind of trouble staying organized when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail myself from being little, but my mom actually kept some of my elementary school papers and notes from teachers, and she’s shown them to me before. A lot of them say things like I was always getting out of my seat, or I’d talk before I was called on, or I’d jump into things too fast during group activities.\n\nThe funny thing is, it wasn’t usually that I didn’t understand the work. Yeah, mostly it shows up with anything that needs, like, a system. The clearest example is still school and my shop, because those are the biggest things I’m juggling right now, but it’s the same kind of problem underneath.

Like, I’ll have supplies for the accessories shop in one place, receipts somewhere else, and then my school due dates or assignment notes end up mixed into the same piles. I’ll think I’m being organized because I put everything “together,” but then later I can’t actually find what I need or figure out what I’m supposed to do first. It’s not just messy-looking, it’s more like my brain can’t sort the categories once they’re all in front of me.

And then I waste a lot of time moving things around, or I’ll start with the shop stuff and suddenly see a school note and switch to that, and then I remember a receipt I was supposed to keep, and it just kind of turns into this big pile of half-sorted things. So, yes, I guess anywhere I have to keep track of different pieces at the same time, it becomes a problem.

(Note: In adolescents or adults, may be limited to feeling restless.)\n  manifestation: Even when I'm not actually going anywhere, I feel like my body is buzzing and I need to be moving, especially when I'm supposed to sit still and listen.\n- key: S016\n  name: Blurting_Answers\n  description: Often blurts out an answer before a question has been completed.\n  manifestation: In group critiques or class discussions, I jump in with my answer before the other person is done talking, and then I feel embarrassed because I realize I guessed what they were going to say.\n- key: S017\n  name: Waiting_Turn_Difficulty\n  description: Often has difficulty waiting his or her turn.\n  manifestation: Waiting for my turn to speak in critiques, stand in financial aid lines, or ask a question after class makes me so impatient that I keep shifting around and sometimes cut in before I mean to.\n- key: additional_requirement_1\n  name: Additional Requirement 1\n  description: Onset prior to age 12 years\n  manifestation: My mom still has elementary school notes saying I was always out of my seat, talking before being called on, and rushing through group activities even when I understood the work.\n- key: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: Because of all this, I missed required steps for my program and my financial aid is now at risk, and people I work with on shop projects have started depending on me less.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 2 years and 2 months\n  manifestation: This has been a real problem for about 2 years and 2 months, since I changed schools and had to manage the business program and my accessories shop with less structure.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S005\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I came in because I feel like I’m kind of drowning in trying to keep things organized. Like, I’m doing schoolwork, and I also have this little accessories shop, and I can’t seem to keep both of them straight at the same time.\n\nIt sounds simple when I say it, but it’s like… my supplies for the shop are in one pile, then receipts are somewhere else, and then my assignment notes and due dates get mixed in with everything. I’ll write something down and then forget where I put it, or I’ll have all these things in front of me and I genuinely don’t know what I’m supposed to do first. Then I just move stuff around or start one thing and remember another thing, and it all gets messy again.\n\nIt’s been really frustrating because I’m trying to handle it, but it feels like my school stuff and my shop stuff are constantly spilling into each other. I think that’s the main reason I wanted to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Did you have the same kind of trouble staying organized when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail myself from being little, but my mom actually kept some of my elementary school papers and notes from teachers, and she’s shown them to me before. A lot of them say things like I was always getting out of my seat, or I’d talk before I was called on, or I’d jump into things too fast during group activities.\n\nThe funny thing is, it wasn’t usually that I didn’t understand the work. From what my mom says, and from the notes, I’d know what to do, but I’d rush through it or kind of take over in the group before listening to the directions all the way. So, yeah, looking back, it seems like there were signs even then, especially at school.\n\nDoctor: Do you have this same trouble staying organized in other parts of your life besides school and your shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly it shows up with anything that needs, like, a system. The clearest example is still school and my shop, because those are the biggest things I’m juggling right now, but it’s the same kind of problem underneath.\n\nLike, I’ll have supplies for the accessories shop in one place, receipts somewhere else, and then my school due dates or assignment notes end up mixed into the same piles. I’ll think I’m being organized because I put everything “together,” but then later I can’t actually find what I need or figure out what I’m supposed to do first. It’s not just messy-looking, it’s more like my brain can’t sort the categories once they’re all in front of me.\n\nAnd then I waste a lot of time moving things around, or I’ll start with the shop stuff and suddenly see a school note and switch to that, and then I remember a receipt I was supposed to keep, and it just kind of turns into this big pile of half-sorted things. So, yes, I guess anywhere I have to keep track of different pieces at the same time, it becomes a problem.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you often lose things you need for school or your shop?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, that happens a lot. It’s frustrating because I’ll sit down actually wanting to finish something, like in class or if I’m watching a video for one of my business assignments, and at first I’m listening. I’ll be like, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I need this, this is important.

But then after a few minutes, I’ll realize my mind has gone somewhere completely different. Like I’ll be thinking about charm designs, or an order idea, or how I could package something differently, and then suddenly I’m like… wait, what did they just say? And I’ve missed half of the explanation.

It’s not always because I don’t care, because sometimes I really do want to finish it and understand it. It’s more like my attention just slips away before I notice it happened. Then I have to rewind the video or ask myself what part of the class I stopped following, and that makes everything take longer.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I think it does, but I guess the bigger thing is that it’s starting to have consequences outside of just, like, “my room is messy” or “I forgot to do a chore.”

With school and my program, I missed some required steps because I didn’t keep track of what had to be done and when. It wasn’t that I was trying to ignore it, I just kept thinking I’d go back and sort it out, and then it got away from me. Now my financial aid is at risk, which is really scary, because that’s not just a small inconvenience. That’s the kind of thing where I’m like, okay, this is actually affecting my life in a serious way.

And with my shop stuff, it’s also changed how people see me. People I’ve worked with on projects have started depending on me less, I think because I’ll miss pieces of the process or not be as on top of things as they need me to be. That feels embarrassing, because I want to be reliable, and in my head I do care about it a lot. But then the follow-through and keeping everything organized doesn’t match what I meant to do.

I’ll write something down and then forget where I put it, or I’ll have all these things in front of me and I genuinely don’t know what I’m supposed to do first. I mean, I don’t remember every detail myself from being little, but my mom actually kept some of my elementary school papers and notes from teachers, and she’s shown them to me before. A lot of them say things like I was always getting out of my seat, or I’d talk before I was called on, or I’d jump into things too fast during group activities.\n\nThe funny thing is, it wasn’t usually that I didn’t understand the work. The clearest example is still school and my shop, because those are the biggest things I’m juggling right now, but it’s the same kind of problem underneath.\n\nLike, I’ll have supplies for the accessories shop in one place, receipts somewhere else, and then my school due dates or assignment notes end up mixed into the same piles. I’ll think I’m being organized because I put everything “together,” but then later I can’t actually find what I need or figure out what I’m supposed to do first. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Um… I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d say I “often” forget everyday tasks in a clear way, because I don’t really keep track of that as its own thing.

Sometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I need to do that later,” and then later I’m not sure if I forgot or if I just put it off because something else came up, if that makes sense. So it’s hard for me to separate forgetting from just not getting around to it right away.

If you mean things like basic daily stuff, I’d probably need examples, because nothing is jumping out at me as something I’m constantly forgetting. I’m not saying it never happens, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a strong pattern with that specifically.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I came in because I feel like I’m kind of drowning in trying to keep things organized. Like, I’m doing schoolwork, and I also have this little accessories shop, and I can’t seem to keep both of them straight at the same time.\n\nIt sounds simple when I say it, but it’s like… my supplies for the shop are in one pile, then receipts are somewhere else, and then my assignment notes and due dates get mixed in with everything. I’ll write something down and then forget where I put it, or I’ll have all these things in front of me and I genuinely don’t know what I’m supposed to do first. Then I just move stuff around or start one thing and remember another thing, and it all gets messy again.\n\nIt’s been really frustrating because I’m trying to handle it, but it feels like my school stuff and my shop stuff are constantly spilling into each other. I think that’s the main reason I wanted to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Did you have the same kind of trouble staying organized when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail myself from being little, but my mom actually kept some of my elementary school papers and notes from teachers, and she’s shown them to me before. A lot of them say things like I was always getting out of my seat, or I’d talk before I was called on, or I’d jump into things too fast during group activities.\n\nThe funny thing is, it wasn’t usually that I didn’t understand the work. From what my mom says, and from the notes, I’d know what to do, but I’d rush through it or kind of take over in the group before listening to the directions all the way. So, yeah, looking back, it seems like there were signs even then, especially at school.\n\nDoctor: Do you have this same trouble staying organized in other parts of your life besides school and your shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly it shows up with anything that needs, like, a system. The clearest example is still school and my shop, because those are the biggest things I’m juggling right now, but it’s the same kind of problem underneath.\n\nLike, I’ll have supplies for the accessories shop in one place, receipts somewhere else, and then my school due dates or assignment notes end up mixed into the same piles. I’ll think I’m being organized because I put everything “together,” but then later I can’t actually find what I need or figure out what I’m supposed to do first. It’s not just messy-looking, it’s more like my brain can’t sort the categories once they’re all in front of me.\n\nAnd then I waste a lot of time moving things around, or I’ll start with the shop stuff and suddenly see a school note and switch to that, and then I remember a receipt I was supposed to keep, and it just kind of turns into this big pile of half-sorted things. So, yes, I guess anywhere I have to keep track of different pieces at the same time, it becomes a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for school or your shop?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I definitely misplace things in the mess sometimes, like I’ll be looking for a note or a receipt and it’s not where I thought it was, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m constantly losing important things.\n\nIt’s more like things get buried or mixed in with other stuff, and then I spend a while digging around trying to figure out where I put them. So… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really been tracking it as “losing” things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task, even when you want to finish it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. It’s frustrating because I’ll sit down actually wanting to finish something, like in class or if I’m watching a video for one of my business assignments, and at first I’m listening. I’ll be like, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I need this, this is important.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, I’ll realize my mind has gone somewhere completely different. Like I’ll be thinking about charm designs, or an order idea, or how I could package something differently, and then suddenly I’m like… wait, what did they just say? And I’ve missed half of the explanation.\n\nIt’s not always because I don’t care, because sometimes I really do want to finish it and understand it. It’s more like my attention just slips away before I notice it happened. Then I have to rewind the video or ask myself what part of the class I stopped following, and that makes everything take longer.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and organization problems also interfere with things like chores or managing your home life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, but I guess the bigger thing is that it’s starting to have consequences outside of just, like, “my room is messy” or “I forgot to do a chore.”\n\nWith school and my program, I missed some required steps because I didn’t keep track of what had to be done and when. It wasn’t that I was trying to ignore it, I just kept thinking I’d go back and sort it out, and then it got away from me. Now my financial aid is at risk, which is really scary, because that’s not just a small inconvenience. That’s the kind of thing where I’m like, okay, this is actually affecting my life in a serious way.\n\nAnd with my shop stuff, it’s also changed how people see me. People I’ve worked with on projects have started depending on me less, I think because I’ll miss pieces of the process or not be as on top of things as they need me to be. That feels embarrassing, because I want to be reliable, and in my head I do care about it a lot. But then the follow-through and keeping everything organized doesn’t match what I meant to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday tasks, even when you meant to do them?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d say I “often” forget everyday tasks in a clear way, because I don’t really keep track of that as its own thing.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I need to do that later,” and then later I’m not sure if I forgot or if I just put it off because something else came up, if that makes sense. So it’s hard for me to separate forgetting from just not getting around to it right away.\n\nIf you mean things like basic daily stuff, I’d probably need examples, because nothing is jumping out at me as something I’m constantly forgetting. So it’s hard for me to separate forgetting from just not getting around to it right away.\n\nIf you mean things like basic daily stuff, I’d probably need examples, because nothing is jumping out at me as something I’m constantly forgetting. I’m not saying it never happens, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a strong pattern with that specifically.\n\n[Last Question]\nDo you get unwanted repetitive thoughts or feel driven to repeat certain behaviors, like checking or arranging things, to relieve anxiety?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 21 / not_in_workforce / high_school\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, at least. Like, I don’t feel like I have to check something over and over or arrange things a certain way so that my anxiety goes down.

I mean, sometimes I’ll double-check normal things, like if I’m unsure whether I did something, but it doesn’t feel like this repetitive urge where I’m stuck doing it or something bad will happen if I don’t. And I don’t really have, like, a specific thought that keeps attacking me over and over where I have to do a behavior to make it stop.

I guess I’d say I haven’t noticed that pattern for myself. If you mean something more specific, I can think about it, but nothing like that is coming to mind.

Now my financial aid is at risk, which is really scary, because that’s not just a small inconvenience. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail from being that young, obviously, but my mom actually still has some of the notes from elementary school, and they’re kind of… embarrassingly consistent.

They’d say things like I was always getting out of my seat, or talking before I was called on. And not just once in a while, but enough that teachers were writing it down and sending notes home. There were also comments about me rushing through group activities, even when I understood the work. Like, it wasn’t that I couldn’t do it or didn’t know what was going on — I’d just move too fast or jump ahead or interrupt the process.

So yeah, looking back, it was already showing up before I was 12. I don’t know that I would’ve described it as “attention and organization” at the time, because as a kid it was more like teachers saying I was too talkative or couldn’t stay in my seat, but it definitely seems connected now.

I’m not saying it never happens, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a strong pattern with that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted repetitive thoughts or feel driven to repeat certain behaviors, like checking or arranging things, to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, at least. Like, I don’t feel like I have to check something over and over or arrange things a certain way so that my anxiety goes down.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll double-check normal things, like if I’m unsure whether I did something, but it doesn’t feel like this repetitive urge where I’m stuck doing it or something bad will happen if I don’t. And I don’t really have, like, a specific thought that keeps attacking me over and over where I have to do a behavior to make it stop.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed that pattern for myself. If you mean something more specific, I can think about it, but nothing like that is coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you already have these kinds of attention and organization problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail from being that young, obviously, but my mom actually still has some of the notes from elementary school, and they’re kind of… embarrassingly consistent.\n\nThey’d say things like I was always getting out of my seat, or talking before I was called on. And not just once in a while, but enough that teachers were writing it down and sending notes home. There were also comments about me rushing through group activities, even when I understood the work. Like, it wasn’t that I couldn’t do it or didn’t know what was going on — I’d just move too fast or jump ahead or interrupt the process.\n\nSo yeah, looking back, it was already showing up before I was 12. I don’t know that I would’ve described it as “attention and organization” at the time, because as a kid it was more like teachers saying I was too talkative or couldn’t stay in my seat, but it definitely seems connected now.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. If the question touches several topics, return the one it is\n  most directly about — never more than one.\n- Copy `key`, `name` and `description` exactly as they appear in [Symptom Profiles].\n- If your profile does not cover what the doctor asked, return the text \"None\" for `key`,\n  `name` and `description`, and say why in `reason`. Do NOT force an unrelated item.\n- On your first reply of the consultation, always return the item whose key is your\n  chief_complaint_symptom_code, whatever the doctor opened with.\n- Do not answer the doctor, and do not write anything outside the JSON.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"key\": \"<key>\", \"name\": \"<name>\", \"description\": \"<description>\", \"reason\": \"<why this item>\"}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "patient_turn_index: 10\n(If 1, this is your first reply — return your chief complaint.)\nReturn the JSON now."
  }

I’m not really sure I’d say “depressed most days,” no. Like, I definitely have moods where I feel blah or kind of frustrated with myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as being depressed.

What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: Um, hi. I guess I came in because I feel like I’m kind of drowning in trying to keep things organized. Like, I’m doing schoolwork, and I also have this little accessories shop, and I can’t seem to keep both of them straight at the same time.\n\nIt sounds simple when I say it, but it’s like… my supplies for the shop are in one pile, then receipts are somewhere else, and then my assignment notes and due dates get mixed in with everything. I’ll write something down and then forget where I put it, or I’ll have all these things in front of me and I genuinely don’t know what I’m supposed to do first. Then I just move stuff around or start one thing and remember another thing, and it all gets messy again.\n\nIt’s been really frustrating because I’m trying to handle it, but it feels like my school stuff and my shop stuff are constantly spilling into each other. I think that’s the main reason I wanted to talk to someone.\n\nDoctor: Did you have the same kind of trouble staying organized when you were a child?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail myself from being little, but my mom actually kept some of my elementary school papers and notes from teachers, and she’s shown them to me before. A lot of them say things like I was always getting out of my seat, or I’d talk before I was called on, or I’d jump into things too fast during group activities.\n\nThe funny thing is, it wasn’t usually that I didn’t understand the work. From what my mom says, and from the notes, I’d know what to do, but I’d rush through it or kind of take over in the group before listening to the directions all the way. So, yeah, looking back, it seems like there were signs even then, especially at school.\n\nDoctor: Do you have this same trouble staying organized in other parts of your life besides school and your shop?\n\nPatient: Yeah, mostly it shows up with anything that needs, like, a system. The clearest example is still school and my shop, because those are the biggest things I’m juggling right now, but it’s the same kind of problem underneath.\n\nLike, I’ll have supplies for the accessories shop in one place, receipts somewhere else, and then my school due dates or assignment notes end up mixed into the same piles. I’ll think I’m being organized because I put everything “together,” but then later I can’t actually find what I need or figure out what I’m supposed to do first. It’s not just messy-looking, it’s more like my brain can’t sort the categories once they’re all in front of me.\n\nAnd then I waste a lot of time moving things around, or I’ll start with the shop stuff and suddenly see a school note and switch to that, and then I remember a receipt I was supposed to keep, and it just kind of turns into this big pile of half-sorted things. So, yes, I guess anywhere I have to keep track of different pieces at the same time, it becomes a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for school or your shop?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I definitely misplace things in the mess sometimes, like I’ll be looking for a note or a receipt and it’s not where I thought it was, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m constantly losing important things.\n\nIt’s more like things get buried or mixed in with other stuff, and then I spend a while digging around trying to figure out where I put them. So… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really been tracking it as “losing” things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task, even when you want to finish it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. It’s frustrating because I’ll sit down actually wanting to finish something, like in class or if I’m watching a video for one of my business assignments, and at first I’m listening. I’ll be like, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I need this, this is important.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, I’ll realize my mind has gone somewhere completely different. Like I’ll be thinking about charm designs, or an order idea, or how I could package something differently, and then suddenly I’m like… wait, what did they just say? And I’ve missed half of the explanation.\n\nIt’s not always because I don’t care, because sometimes I really do want to finish it and understand it. It’s more like my attention just slips away before I notice it happened. Then I have to rewind the video or ask myself what part of the class I stopped following, and that makes everything take longer.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and organization problems also interfere with things like chores or managing your home life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, but I guess the bigger thing is that it’s starting to have consequences outside of just, like, “my room is messy” or “I forgot to do a chore.”\n\nWith school and my program, I missed some required steps because I didn’t keep track of what had to be done and when. It wasn’t that I was trying to ignore it, I just kept thinking I’d go back and sort it out, and then it got away from me. Now my financial aid is at risk, which is really scary, because that’s not just a small inconvenience. That’s the kind of thing where I’m like, okay, this is actually affecting my life in a serious way.\n\nAnd with my shop stuff, it’s also changed how people see me. People I’ve worked with on projects have started depending on me less, I think because I’ll miss pieces of the process or not be as on top of things as they need me to be. That feels embarrassing, because I want to be reliable, and in my head I do care about it a lot. But then the follow-through and keeping everything organized doesn’t match what I meant to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday tasks, even when you meant to do them?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d say I “often” forget everyday tasks in a clear way, because I don’t really keep track of that as its own thing.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I need to do that later,” and then later I’m not sure if I forgot or if I just put it off because something else came up, if that makes sense. So it’s hard for me to separate forgetting from just not getting around to it right away.\n\nIf you mean things like basic daily stuff, I’d probably need examples, because nothing is jumping out at me as something I’m constantly forgetting. I’m not saying it never happens, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a strong pattern with that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted repetitive thoughts or feel driven to repeat certain behaviors, like checking or arranging things, to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, at least. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I don’t think so… not in a way where I’d call it excessive, at least. I mean, I worry about things like anyone does — like if something important is coming up, or if I’m unsure about what I’m supposed to be doing, I might think about it a lot for a bit. But I don’t feel like I’m constantly worrying about every area of my life all day, if that’s what you mean.

Sometimes I’m not totally sure how to judge what counts as “a lot,” because if you asked someone else maybe they’d say I overthink certain things, but to me it doesn’t feel like this ongoing, uncontrollable worry about multiple topics. It’s more situational, I guess. Like it depends on what’s happening, and then it usually passes or gets replaced by whatever else is going on.

So… I’d say no, I haven’t really noticed a pattern of excessive worrying across different parts of my life.

The clearest example is still school and my shop, because those are the biggest things I’m juggling right now, but it’s the same kind of problem underneath.\n\nLike, I’ll have supplies for the accessories shop in one place, receipts somewhere else, and then my school due dates or assignment notes end up mixed into the same piles. I’ll think I’m being organized because I put everything “together,” but then later I can’t actually find what I need or figure out what I’m supposed to do first. It’s not just messy-looking, it’s more like my brain can’t sort the categories once they’re all in front of me.\n\nAnd then I waste a lot of time moving things around, or I’ll start with the shop stuff and suddenly see a school note and switch to that, and then I remember a receipt I was supposed to keep, and it just kind of turns into this big pile of half-sorted things. So, yes, I guess anywhere I have to keep track of different pieces at the same time, it becomes a problem.\n\nDoctor: Do you often lose things you need for school or your shop?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not totally sure how to answer that. I mean, I definitely misplace things in the mess sometimes, like I’ll be looking for a note or a receipt and it’s not where I thought it was, but I don’t know if I’d say I’m constantly losing important things.\n\nIt’s more like things get buried or mixed in with other stuff, and then I spend a while digging around trying to figure out where I put them. So… maybe sometimes, but I haven’t really been tracking it as “losing” things, if that makes sense.\n\nDoctor: Do you often have trouble staying focused on a task, even when you want to finish it?\n\nPatient: Yeah, that happens a lot. It’s frustrating because I’ll sit down actually wanting to finish something, like in class or if I’m watching a video for one of my business assignments, and at first I’m listening. I’ll be like, okay, I’m going to pay attention, I need this, this is important.\n\nBut then after a few minutes, I’ll realize my mind has gone somewhere completely different. Like I’ll be thinking about charm designs, or an order idea, or how I could package something differently, and then suddenly I’m like… wait, what did they just say? And I’ve missed half of the explanation.\n\nIt’s not always because I don’t care, because sometimes I really do want to finish it and understand it. It’s more like my attention just slips away before I notice it happened. Then I have to rewind the video or ask myself what part of the class I stopped following, and that makes everything take longer.\n\nDoctor: Do these attention and organization problems also interfere with things like chores or managing your home life?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think it does, but I guess the bigger thing is that it’s starting to have consequences outside of just, like, “my room is messy” or “I forgot to do a chore.”\n\nWith school and my program, I missed some required steps because I didn’t keep track of what had to be done and when. It wasn’t that I was trying to ignore it, I just kept thinking I’d go back and sort it out, and then it got away from me. Now my financial aid is at risk, which is really scary, because that’s not just a small inconvenience. That’s the kind of thing where I’m like, okay, this is actually affecting my life in a serious way.\n\nAnd with my shop stuff, it’s also changed how people see me. People I’ve worked with on projects have started depending on me less, I think because I’ll miss pieces of the process or not be as on top of things as they need me to be. That feels embarrassing, because I want to be reliable, and in my head I do care about it a lot. But then the follow-through and keeping everything organized doesn’t match what I meant to do.\n\nDoctor: Do you often forget everyday tasks, even when you meant to do them?\n\nPatient: Um… I’m not sure, honestly. Like, I don’t know if I’d say I “often” forget everyday tasks in a clear way, because I don’t really keep track of that as its own thing.\n\nSometimes I’ll think, “Oh, I need to do that later,” and then later I’m not sure if I forgot or if I just put it off because something else came up, if that makes sense. So it’s hard for me to separate forgetting from just not getting around to it right away.\n\nIf you mean things like basic daily stuff, I’d probably need examples, because nothing is jumping out at me as something I’m constantly forgetting. I’m not saying it never happens, but I don’t know that I’ve noticed a strong pattern with that specifically.\n\nDoctor: Do you get unwanted repetitive thoughts or feel driven to repeat certain behaviors, like checking or arranging things, to relieve anxiety?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, at least. Like, I don’t feel like I have to check something over and over or arrange things a certain way so that my anxiety goes down.\n\nI mean, sometimes I’ll double-check normal things, like if I’m unsure whether I did something, but it doesn’t feel like this repetitive urge where I’m stuck doing it or something bad will happen if I don’t. And I don’t really have, like, a specific thought that keeps attacking me over and over where I have to do a behavior to make it stop.\n\nI guess I’d say I haven’t noticed that pattern for myself. If you mean something more specific, I can think about it, but nothing like that is coming to mind.\n\nDoctor: Before age 12, did you already have these kinds of attention and organization problems?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I think so. I mean, I don’t remember every detail from being that young, obviously, but my mom actually still has some of the notes from elementary school, and they’re kind of… embarrassingly consistent.\n\nThey’d say things like I was always getting out of my seat, or talking before I was called on. And not just once in a while, but enough that teachers were writing it down and sending notes home. There were also comments about me rushing through group activities, even when I understood the work. Like, it wasn’t that I couldn’t do it or didn’t know what was going on — I’d just move too fast or jump ahead or interrupt the process.\n\nSo yeah, looking back, it was already showing up before I was 12. I don’t know that I would’ve described it as “attention and organization” at the time, because as a kid it was more like teachers saying I was too talkative or couldn’t stay in my seat, but it definitely seems connected now.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed most days lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure I’d say “depressed most days,” no. Like, I definitely have moods where I feel blah or kind of frustrated with myself, but I don’t know if that’s the same thing as being depressed.\n\nLately I haven’t noticed a clear pattern of waking up every day feeling really down, or feeling like that’s the main thing going on. If anything, I’d probably need to think about what you mean by depressed, because I sometimes use that word casually, but clinically… I’m not sure it fits. So I guess my answer is, not that I’ve noticed as a most-days thing.\n\nDoctor: Do you spend a lot of time worrying excessively about different parts of your life?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way where I’d call it excessive, at least. I mean, I worry about things like anyone does — like if something important is coming up, or if I’m unsure about what I’m supposed to be doing, I might think about it a lot for a bit. But I don’t feel like I’m constantly worrying about every area of my life all day, if that’s what you mean.\n\nSometimes I’m not totally sure how to judge what counts as “a lot,” because if you asked someone else maybe they’d say I overthink certain things, but to me it doesn’t feel like this ongoing, uncontrollable worry about multiple topics. It’s more situational, I guess. Like it depends on what’s happening, and then it usually passes or gets replaced by whatever else is going on.\n\nSo… I’d say no, I haven’t really noticed a pattern of excessive worrying across different parts of my life."
  }

Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nUm, hi. I guess I came in because I feel like I’m kind of drowning in trying to keep things organized. Like, I’m doing schoolwork, and I also have this little accessories shop, and I can’t seem to keep both of them straight at the same time.\n\nIt sounds simple when I say it, but it’s like… my supplies for the shop are in one pile, then receipts are somewhere else, and then my assignment notes and due dates get mixed in with everything. I’ll write something down and then forget where I put it, or I’ll have all these things in front of me and I genuinely don’t know what I’m supposed to do first. Then I just move stuff around or start one thing and remember another thing, and it all gets messy again.\n\nIt’s been really frustrating because I’m trying to handle it, but it feels like my school stuff and my shop stuff are constantly spilling into each other. I think that’s the main reason I wanted to talk to someone.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt.  Subtypes: Recurrent Thoughts of Death, Suicidal Ideation, Suicidal Impulse, Specific Plan for Suicide, Suicide Attempt\n[S083] Hopelessness: A feeling of despair or having no hope for the future.  Subtypes: Future Hopelessness, Belief in Impossibility of Improvement, Helpless Resignation, Chronic Pessimism\n[S033] Elevated_Irritable_Mood: A distinct period of abnormally and persistently elevated, expansive, or irritable mood.  Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions).  Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging). Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. Like I’ll be thinking about charm designs, or an order idea, or how I could package something differently, and then suddenly I’m like… wait, what did they just say? And I’ve missed half of the explanation.\n\nIt’s not always because I don’t care, because sometimes I really do want to finish it and understand it. It’s more like my attention just slips away before I notice it happened. Then I have to rewind the video or ask myself what part of the class I stopped following, and that makes everything take longer.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [judge] ==========
{"confirmed":["S002","S008"],"denied":[],"reasoning":{"S002":"The patient describes repeatedly losing focus after a few minutes during class or assignment videos despite wanting to pay attention and understand the material.","S008":"The patient reports their mind drifting to unrelated thoughts such as charm designs, order ideas, and packaging, causing them to miss the explanation."}}

Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame). Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings). Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah… I think it does, but I guess the bigger thing is that it’s starting to have consequences outside of just, like, “my room is messy” or “I forgot to do a chore.”\n\nWith school and my program, I missed some required steps because I didn’t keep track of what had to be done and when. Now my financial aid is at risk, which is really scary, because that’s not just a small inconvenience. Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others. Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.
